The Constitution of Serbia recognizes two [autonomous provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Serbia#Autonomous_provinces): [Vojvodina](176801.md) in the north and the [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Kosovo) territory of [Kosovo](17391.md) in the south, while the remaining area of [Central Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Serbia) has never had its own regional authority. Vojvodina has its own assembly and government, but with limited competencies, focused primarily on executive power, without substantive legislative jurisdiction. Following the [Kosovo War](16760.md), the Serbian state apparatus, including its military and police forces, was withdrawn from Kosovo, leading to the establishment of the [UN interim administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Interim_Administration_Mission_in_Kosovo). In 2008, Kosovo [declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Kosovo_declaration_of_independence), a move strongly opposed by Serbia, which considers the declaration illegal and illegitimate and continues to claim territory as its province, officially known as [Kosovo and Metohija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Province_of_Kosovo_and_Metohija).[^cite_note-172] The declaration received a mixed response from the international community, with [110 out of 193 UN member states](15808914.md) recognizing its independence.[^cite_note-173]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Serbia)

Serbia has established diplomatic relations with 189 [UN member states](31969.md) and two [non-member observer states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_observers).[^cite_note-DList-174] It maintains a network of 78 embassies abroad and seven diplomatic missions to multilateral organizations, while hosting the embassies of 72 countries.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] Serbia is a member of the [United Nations](31769.md), the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md), and the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), among other international organizations.&#x20;

Country has pursued a [multi-vector policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-Vector_Policy), balancing its relations with the European Union, Russia, the United States, and China.[^cite_note-Bieber2023-177][^cite_note-178]&#x20;

Serbia’s strategic goal remains eventual membership in the [European Union](9317.md) and the country has been negotiating its EU accession since 2014.[^cite_note-179] Its [accession process](10038148.md) has been slow, with 22 of 35 negotiation chapters opened and only a few provisionally closed. Key obstacles include the "normalization" of relations with [Kosovo](17391.md), alignment of its foreign policy with that of the EU regarding Russia, and rule of law reforms.[^cite_note-180] After initially enjoying strong public support, Serbia's EU accession has faced declining domestic approval over the past decade.[^cite_note-181] Support for Serbia's accession among individual EU member states is similarly mixed, with concerns over Serbia's claim to Kosovo and its foreign policy non-alignment with regard to Russia.[^cite_note-182]&#x20;



&#x20; States that have not recognised Kosovo as independent

&#x20; States that have recognised Kosovo as independent

&#x20; States that have recognised Kosovo as independent and later withdrawn recognition

Relations with [Russia](25391.md) are often described as a "centuries-old religious, ethnic and political alliance".[^cite_note-183] Russia has consistently provided diplomatic support to Serbia over the issue of Kosovo. Following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Serbia drew international scrutiny for not joining [international sanctions against Russia](70323386.md), citing its own past suffering with sanctions.[^cite_note-Bieber2023-177] It is the only European country, apart from Belarus, not to impose sanctions on Russia.[^cite_note-AP_NEWS_2023-184] However, Serbia voted to condemn the invasion, supporting the adoption of the [UN resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/1) demanding Russia to withdraw its military forces from Ukraine, as well as supporting the suspension of Russia from the [UN Human Rights Council](635790.md).[^cite_note-Bieber2023-177][^cite_note-Mojsilović_2022-185]&#x20;

Country maintains close diplomatic ties with [China](5405.md), with the two countries having developed a comprehensive strategic partnership.[^cite_note-186] China has become a major creditor for infrastructure projects through the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative), while Chinese companies have invested heavily in Serbia, using the country as one of several springboards for expansion into European markets.[^cite_note-187] Serbia follows the [One China principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_China_principle) and considers Taiwan an "inalienable part" of China.[^cite_note-188] Similarly, China provides strong diplomatic support to Serbia on the issue of Kosovo.[^cite_note-189]&#x20;

Relations with the [United States](3434750.md) have improved significantly from the low point of 1999 and the [NATO bombing](322473.md), although Serbia's refusal to recognize Kosovo's independence remains a point of contention, as does its balancing act between Western and non-Western powers, particularly Russia and China.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]&#x20;

Serbia's relations with neighboring countries are generally friendly, although ties with some of the former Yugoslav republics, particularly Croatia and Bosnia-Herzegovina, remain burdened by the legacy of the [breakup of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakup_of_Yugoslavia) and subsequent [wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars), as well as differing historical interpretations of those events. Bilateral ties with [Croatia](5573.md) are frequently strained by unresolved legacy issues from the [war in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence).[^cite_note-192] Relations with [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md) are complex and center around questions of Bosnia and Herzegovina's internal sovereignty, owing to Serbia's close ties with [Republic of Srpska](347422.md), the Serb-majority entity within Bosnia and Herzegovina with which Serbia maintains institutionalized special relations.[^cite_note-193] Relations with [Montenegro](20760.md) are complicated by the status of the Serbian language, which, despite being the mother tongue of a relative majority of the population, is not recognized as an official language.[^cite_note-194]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Serbian Armed Forces](381170.md)

The [Serbian Armed Forces](381170.md) are the military of Serbia and are composed of the [Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Army) and the [Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force_and_Air_Defence). The Army consists of four mixed (mechanized infantry and armored) brigades, an artillery brigade and [River Flotilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_River_Flotilla), which patrols the Danube, Sava and Tisza rivers; the Air Force consists of two air brigades, an air defence brigade, and an air surveillance brigade. In addition, there are units attached to the [General Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_General_Staff), including two special forces brigades, a signal brigade, and a logistics brigade.&#x20;



[MiG-29](185653.md) of [Serbian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force_and_Air_Defence) carrying [CM-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CM-400) supersonic ballistic missiles

The armed forces consist of about 22,500 active military personnel: 13,200 in the Army, 3,000 in the Air Force, and 6,300 in units attached to the General Staff.[^cite_note-auto-195] They are supplemented by the "active" [reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_reserve_force), with about 2,000 members who are generally required to perform 45 days of military service per year, and the "passive" reserve, which totals about 600,000 citizens with past military training and is activated only in the event of war.[^cite_note-novosti-196][^cite_note-197] [Conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Serbia) with six months of mandatory military service was abolished in 2011 when Serbia switched to all-volunteer professional armed forces.[^cite_note-198] However, reintroduction of conscription is planned for 2027, requiring all males who have reached the age of eighteen and are found fit for service to serve 75 days.[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

In 2026, total defence spending amounted to \$2.7 billion, equivalent to 2.5% of the country's GDP.[^cite_note-200] That same year, the Serbian Armed Forces ranked 63rd out of 145 militaries assessed in the annual Global Firepower Military Strength Ranking.[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

The [President](774382.md) serves as [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) and appoints the [Chief of the General Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Serbian_General_Staff), who exercises operational command over the armed forces through the General Staff.[^cite_note-202] The [Government](608371.md), through the [Minister of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Serbia\)), is responsible for defence policy and spending, ensuring [civil control of the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_control_of_the_military).[^cite_note-203]&#x20;

Within its self-declared status of permanent [military neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country), Serbia has no intention of joining a military alliance, including [NATO](21133.md), of which most neighboring countries are members, due to [strong popular opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia%E2%80%93NATO_relations#Opinion_polling_on_NATO_membership), largely a legacy of the [NATO bombing of Yugoslavia](322473.md) in 1999.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205] It has a tradition of engaging in [UN peacekeeping missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_Nations_peacekeeping_missions), including current deployments in Lebanon, Cyprus, Ivory Coast, and Liberia.[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Serbia)

Serbia is an upper-middle-income [developing](78449.md) [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy).[^cite_note-207] It is predominantly a [service economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy), with the [tertiary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector) accounting for 58.8% of GDP, followed by the [secondary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_sector) with 26.1% and the [primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector) with 3.2%.[^cite_note-208] The country has a very high level of [human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) and moderate levels of [income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient). The official currency is the [Serbian dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_dinar), and the central bank is the [National Bank of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_of_Serbia).&#x20;

The labour force stands at 3.1 million. The [employment rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_rate) was 50.9% in 2025; the employment structure shows the characteristics of [post-industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-industrial_economy) economies, with 60.1% employed in tertiary sector, 28.6% in secondary sector and 11.3% in the primary sector. [Unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment) remains a problem, with rate of 7.2% as of 2026.[^cite_note-209] The average monthly net [salary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salary) in March 2026 stood at 121,805 dinars or \$1,185 while the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) for 2027 is set at 70,450 dinars or \$690.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211]&#x20;

Serbia has an unfavourable [balance of trade](4810.md): imports exceed exports by 26.6%. Serbia's exports of goods, however, records steady growth reaching \$37.3 billion in 2025.[^cite_note-smt-212] The country is a member of the [CEFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_European_Free_Trade_Agreement), a [free-trade area](10883.md) covering the Western Balkans and Moldova. It has a [preferential trade regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preferential_trading_area) with the [European Union](9317.md) and [EFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association), and individual [free trade agreements](2533562.md) with China, Russia, Turkey, Kazakhstan, and Belarus.[^cite_note-SIEPA_-_Liberalized_Trade-213]&#x20;

Economic development across Serbia varies significantly, with Belgrade, a [beta-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network#Beta) [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city), acting as the country's primary economic hub.[^cite_note-214] Its metropolitan area accounts for 41.7% of the national income, with [gross metropolitan product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_metropolitan_product) of \$46.8 billion in 2025, making Belgrade capital region one of the largest regional economies in the Balkans.[^cite_note-v058-215]&#x20;

### Industry

The manufacturing and mining sectors account for 12.7% and 3% of GDP, respectively.[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_2025-216]&#x20;

Since 2000, more than €20 billion in foreign direct investment has flowed into Serbia's manufacturing and mining sectors. As a result, the country's industrial sector has a strong presence of foreign-owned companies, including numerous major multinational corporations.[^cite_note-217]&#x20;



*[Fiat Grande Panda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiat_Grande_Panda)* manufactured at the [Stellantis](64563392.md) plant in [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac)



*[Avenio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens_Avenio)* tram manufactured at the [Siemens Mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens_Mobility) plant in Kragujevac

[^cite_note-218]

The [automotive industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Serbia) is anchored by the [Stellantis](64563392.md) automobile assembly plant in Kragujevac, which produces the *[Fiat Grande Panda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiat_Grande_Panda)* and *[Citroën C3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citro%C3%ABn_C3)*.[^cite_note-219] The country is also home to dozens of manufacturers of automotive components, including [ZF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZF_Friedrichshafen) (electric drives, power electronics and switches), [Aumovio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aumovio) (digital instrument clusters and infotainment screens), [Bosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosch_\(company\)) (windscreen wiper systems), [Continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_AG) (cooling and heating hoses), [Nidec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nidec) (brushless DC motors and electric power steering motors) and [Yazaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazaki) (electrical wiring), as well as major global tyre manufacturers such as [Michelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin), [Toyo Tires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyo_Tires) and [Linglong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linglong_Tire).[^cite_note-220]&#x20;

Serbia's [mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining) sector is comparatively strong: country is the 16th-largest producer of [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal) (third in Europe) and the 23rd-largest producer of [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper) (third in Europe).[^cite_note-EIA-221][^cite_note-222] Its proven reserves of [coal lignite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lignite) are the fifth-largest in the world (second in Europe).[^cite_note-b92.net-223] Coal is extracted by the national utility company [EPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektroprivreda_Srbije) from two large deposits, [Kolubara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RB_Kolubara) and [Kostolac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostolac_coal_mine).[^cite_note-b92.net-223] Copper is extracted, refined and smelted at the [Bor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bor,_Serbia) mining complex by the Chinese company [Zijin Mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zijin_Mining).[^cite_note-224] Significant gold extraction is carried out around [Majdanpek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdanpek_mine). The [Jadar Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadar_mine) in western Serbia contains one of Europe's largest lithium deposits, with mining rights held by [Rio Tinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Tinto_\(corporation\)); however, exploitation has not begun amid large protests over its potential environmental impact, and the future of the proposed mine remains uncertain.[^cite_note-225]&#x20;

Serbia is a leading [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel) producer in Southeast Europe, with 1.4 million tonnes of crude steel produced in 2025, all at the [Hesteel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesteel_Serbia) steel mill in Smederevo.[^cite_note-226]&#x20;

The [arms industry](608296.md) is well-developed, with Serbia ranked 25th worldwide in arms exports, surpassing \$1.6 billion in 2023.[^cite_note-227] The sector is dominated by domestic companies such as [Yugoimport–SDPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoimport%E2%80%93SDPR) (self-propelled howitzers, infantry fighting vehicles and armored vehicles), [Sloboda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sloboda_\(defense_company\)) (artillery ammunition), [Krušik](52360987.md) (large-caliber ammunition), [Milan Blagojević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan_Blagojevi%C4%87_-_Namenska) (gunpowder), [Zastava Arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zastava_Arms) (firearms), and [Prvi Partizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prvi_Partizan) (small-caliber ammunition), as well as SDS Technologies (short-range combat drones), a joint venture between Israeli [Elbit Systems](1341669.md) and Yugoimport-SDPR.[^cite_note-228]&#x20;

Some global brand names in the [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_industry) and [beverage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drink_industry) sectors have established production in Serbia. [Nestlé](160227.md) manufactures plant-based meat products, [PepsiCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PepsiCo) snack foods, and [Dr. Oetker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Oetker) powdered food products, while [Barry Callebaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Callebaut) processes cocoa and manufactures chocolate.[^cite_note-229] [Coca-Cola HBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola_HBC) is the anchor bottler of Coca-Cola for the region.[^cite_note-230] Serbia ranked 43rd globally in beer production in 2023, with three major multinational corporations, [Heineken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heineken), [Carlsberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlsberg_Group) and [Molson Coors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molson_Coors), operating breweries in the country.[^cite_note-231] Major global tobacco producers [Philip Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Morris_International), [British American Tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_American_Tobacco) and [Japan Tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Tobacco) have factories in Serbia.&#x20;

The [chemical industry](58721.md) has a diverse industrial footprint. [Petrohemija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIP-Petrohemija) is the country's only petrochemical complex, producing polymers alongside basic feedstocks such as ethylene and propylene. [Henkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henkel) is a major producer of household chemicals, manufacturing laundry detergents. [Messer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messer_Group) and [Linde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linde_plc) are major suppliers of industrial gases. Mineral fertilizers are produced by the domestic company Elixir.[^cite_note-232] [Hemofarm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemofarm), owned by German [Stada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stada_Arzneimittel), is one of the largest pharmaceutical manufacturers in the region, producing almost 8 billion units of both prescription and over-the-counter medicines per year.[^cite_note-233]&#x20;

The [machine industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_industry) covers a range of products. [Siemens Mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens_Mobility) manufactures *[Avenio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens_Avenio)* trams, while [Grundfos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grundfos) produces pumps.[^cite_note-234] Flender ([de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flender_\(Getriebehersteller\))) manufactures generators for wind turbines, while [Bizerba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bizerba) produces retail scales and [Hansgrohe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hansgrohe) manufactures faucets. [Hisense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisense) produces refrigerators and freezers.[^cite_note-235]&#x20;

The [building materials industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_material) is centered on cement, roofing materials, and floor coverings. [Holcim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holcim_Group), [CRH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRH_plc) and [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_Cement) are major cement producers, while [Wienerberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wienerberger) manufactures roof tiles and [Tarkett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarkett) produces a broad range of floor coverings.[^cite_note-236]&#x20;

Serbia's [packaging industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Packaging) brings together major international manufacturers across several segments. [Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball_Corporation) produces more than 1.5 billion aluminum beverage cans annually, while Vaider Group manufactures glass bottles for beverages.[^cite_note-237] [DS Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DS_Smith) and [Smurfit Westrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smurfit_Westrock) manufacture corrugated cardboard shipping and retail containers, while [Tetra Pak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetra_Pak) produces aseptic cartons for liquid food.[^cite_note-238]&#x20;

### Science and technology

In 2024, Serbia's expenditure on research and development (R\&D) amounted to 1% of GDP, below the European average of over 2%. Serbia was ranked 54th in the Global Innovation Index in 2025.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240]&#x20;

The [information and communications technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_and_communications_technology) sector contributes roughly 8.5% of GDP and has grown into the country's largest export category, with ICT services exports reaching \$5.3 billion in 2025.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242]&#x20;



Science Technology Park in [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1), one of six such centers in Serbia supporting technology [startups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Startup_company)

Serbia has begun to develop [hyperscale computing infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperscale_computing), with one [tier IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_center_tiers) [data center](579730.md) in Kragujevac, which, with a capacity of 14 MW and planned expansion to 54 MW by 2030, is currently the largest [supercomputer](37153.md) cluster in Southeastern Europe.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]&#x20;

Large investments by global technology companies have contributed significantly to the growth of Serbia's technology sector. [Microsoft](19001.md) opened its development center in Belgrade in 2005, the fifth such center in the world at the time, which has since become one of the company's largest software engineering centers in Europe.[^cite_note-245] [Schneider Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schneider_Electric) and [Aumovio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aumovio) also operate global development and software engineering centers in the country, specializing respectively in energy management and electrical systems and in digital display solutions for automobiles. [Oracle](22591.md), [Cisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisco), and [NCR Voyix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCR_Voyix) have also established outsourcing and service centers in Serbia.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]&#x20;

Serbia also has a sizeable [video game industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_game_industry), headed by [Nordeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordeus), a subsidiary of [Take-Two Interactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Take-Two_Interactive) and the developer of *[Top Eleven Football Manager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Eleven_Football_Manager)*, one of the most widely played mobile sports games, with over 300 million registered users worldwide, as well as Trilateral Studio, owned by [Epic Games](573199.md), which specializes in creating realistic 3D characters and facial animations for video games.[^cite_note-248]&#x20;

In last decade, a growing number of domestic [startups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Startup_company) obtained funding from international investors. Ominimo, an AI-driven auto insurance startup, became Serbia's first [unicorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Startup_company#Unicorn) after raising a \$1.6 billion investment in 2026.[^cite_note-249]&#x20;

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Serbia)

Serbia has very favourable natural conditions, including its land and climate, for diverse agricultural production. It has 3,373,034 hectares of utilized [agricultural land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_land), of which 3,294,000 hectares is [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land) (0.5 hectares per capita).[^cite_note-250] Agriculture accounts for 3.2% of the country's GDP.[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_2025-216] In 2024, exports of agricultural and food products stood at \$5.8 billion, with an export-import ratio of 178%.[^cite_note-251]&#x20;



Serbia is one of the largest producers of [raspberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raspberry) and [blackberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackberry)

Large-scale and more industrialized agricultural production is concentrated in the northern part of the country, in the regions of [Bačka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%8Dka), [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat), [Syrmia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrmia) and [Mačva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma%C4%8Dva), which account for the bulk of the country's agricultural output. Agriculture in these areas is characterized by larger farms and extensive cultivation of cereals, oilseeds and other field crops. The rest of the country relies predominantly on smaller-scale farming, with production focused more on fruits and vegetables.[^cite_note-252]&#x20;

The country is a significant producer of cereals, particularly [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize) (6,630,984 tonnes in 2023, ranked 22nd globally) and [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) (3,448,700 tonnes, 33rd globally), root crops such as [sugar beet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_beet) (2,040,624 tonnes, 21st globally), as well as oilseeds such as [sunflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_sunflower) (686,628 tonnes, 16th globally) and [soybean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean) (599,878 tonnes, 16th globally).[^cite_note-faostat-253]&#x20;

Serbia has significant fruit production: in 2023, it was the world's third-largest producer of [raspberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raspberry) (98,674 tonnes), the fourth-largest producer of [plums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum) (362,713 tonnes), and the fifth-largest producer of [sour cherries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_cerasus) (144,849 tonnes). It also has sizeable production of [cherries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry) (15,575 tonnes, 28th worldwide), [apples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple) (486,215 tonnes, 29th globally), [pears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pear) (59,711 tonnes, 29th globally), [watermelons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermelon) (60,959 tonnes, 46th worldwide), and [grapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grape) (131,525 tonnes, 47th globally). The most widely produced vegetable is the [potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato) (599,574 tonnes, 53rd globally). The country also produces significant quantities of [sunflower oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunflower_oil) (204,308 tonnes, 16th globally) and [soybean oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean_oil) (64,270 tonnes, 38th globally).[^cite_note-faostat-253]&#x20;

It has a long tradition of winemaking, with several autochthonous grape varieties still cultivated today. There are 17,437 hectares of vineyards in Serbia, with [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine) production reaching 23,370 tonnes in 2023, ranking 38th worldwide.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-faostat-253]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Serbia)



[Kopaonik ski resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopaonik_ski_resort)



[Golubac Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golubac_Fortress)

Serbia is not a mass-tourism destination with tourism accounting for 3% of country's GDP.[^cite_note-255] In 2024, 4.4 million tourist arrivals were recorded at accommodations, of which 2.3 million were foreign visitors.[^cite_note-256] Foreign exchange earnings from tourism were estimated at about \$3 billion.[^cite_note-257] Tourism is mainly focused on the country's mountain and spa resorts, which are predominantly visited by domestic tourists, while Belgrade is the preferred destination for foreign visitors, accounting for almost two-thirds of all foreign tourist arrivals.[^cite_note-258]&#x20;

Mountain tourism is centred on resorts such as [Kopaonik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopaonik), [Zlatibor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlatibor) and [Stara Planina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stara_Planina_ski_resort), which offer skiing and other winter sports, as well as hiking and nature-based activities during the warmer months. [Spa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spa) tourism is well developed, with spa resort towns such as [Vrnjačka Banja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrnja%C4%8Dka_Banja), [Palić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali%C4%87) and [Banja Koviljača](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banja_Kovilja%C4%8Da) combining thermal and mineral waters with wellness and health-related services.[^cite_note-259]&#x20;

City-break tourism is concentrated primarily in Belgrade, which offers a combination of cultural attractions, gastronomy and nightlife, while also serving as destination for conference and business tourism.[^cite_note-260]&#x20;

River cruising is a growing tourism product, with the [Danube](21209639.md) forming one of Europe's major river-cruise routes. Itineraries connecting Serbia with cities such as Budapest and Vienna typically stop in Belgrade and Novi Sad, as well as at historic fortifications such as [Golubac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golubac_Fortress) and [Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Fortress) along the riverbanks.[^cite_note-261]&#x20;

Festival tourism has developed as a distinct niche, with major music and food festivals attracting visitors from Serbia and abroad. Several internationally renowned music festivals are held in Serbia, including [Exit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exit_\(festival\)) and the [Guča Trumpet Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu%C4%8Da_Trumpet_Festival), alongside numerous food festivals, most notably the [Leskovac Grill Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ro%C5%A1tiljijada).[^cite_note-262]&#x20;

Heritage tourism is another tourism product, centred on the country's [Orthodox monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Serbian_Orthodox_monasteries), Roman archaeological sites, and numerous medieval fortresses.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Serbia](38878355.md)



[Wind farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm) near [Alibunar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alibunar)

Serbia is a net exporter of [electrical energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_energy) as its electricity production reached 34.3 TWh in 2025, compared with final consumption of 31.5 TWh.[^cite_note-energy_agency_of-265] Some 69% of electricity generation comes from [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel), while 31% comes from [renewable sources](25784.md). In 2025, total installed capacity stood at 8,868 MW, comprising six coal-fired [thermal power stations](2549543.md) with a combined capacity of 4,153 MW, fourteen [hydroelectric power stations](381399.md) with 2,948 MW, nine [wind farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_farm) with 828 MW, four natural gas- and mazut-fired thermal power stations with 552 MW, and [photovoltaic power stations](34779206.md) with a combined capacity of 195 MW. Over 90% of electricity production is concentrated in [EPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektroprivreda_Srbije), national [electric utility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_utility) company.[^cite_note-energy_agency_of-265]&#x20;

Country is heavily dependent on foreign sources for [oil](4207510.md) and [natural gas](22131.md). Domestic oil and gas production satisfies only 20% and 10% of the country's needs, respectively. In 2025, the national oil company, [NIS](7562798.md), produced 0.77 million tonnes of oil as well as around 300 million cubic metres of natural gas.[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] The company operates the country's sole oil refinery in [Pančevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan%C4%8Devo), with a capacity of 4.8 million tonnes per year. A branch of the [Adria pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adria_oil_pipeline) connects the Pančevo oil refinery to oil terminals on the Adriatic Sea.[^cite_note-268] A separate Serbia-Hungary oil pipeline, which will be connected to [Druzhba pipeline](1950396.md), is designed to provide an alternative route and is scheduled to become operational in 2027.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] The [Balkan Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Stream), a natural gas pipeline with a capacity of 13.9 billion cubic metres annually, runs through Serbia and carries most of the country's imported natural gas.[^cite_note-271] There is an underground natural gas storage facility at [Banatski Dvor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banatski_Dvor), with a capacity of 750 million cubic metres, equivalent to approximately one-quarter of the country's annual consumption.[^cite_note-272] The natural gas system comprises 3,217 km of trunk pipelines and 25,707 km of distribution pipelines. Around 13% of households are directly heated by natural gas, while a further 22% are supplied with [district heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_heating) generated by gas-fired [heating plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heating_plant).[^cite_note-energy_agency_of-265][^cite_note-273]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Serbia)

Serbian [road network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Serbia) carries the bulk of traffic in the country. Total length of paved roads stands at 31,463 km (19,550 mi) and consist of 13,638 km (8,474 mi) of national road network ("state roads") and 17,825 km (11,076 mi) of local roads ("municipal roads").[^cite_note-274] There are 1,092 km (679 mi) of [motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway) and 222 km (138 mi) of [expressways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_road). The most of the road network, except for motorways and expressways, are of comparatively lower quality to the Western European standards because of lack of financial resources for their maintenance in the previous decades. As of 2025, there were 2,573,843 registered passenger cars or [392 per 1,000 persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_territories_by_motor_vehicles_per_capita).[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of-275] [Coach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_\(bus\)) transport is extensive, with almost every part of the country connected by bus services, from major cities to villages. More than a hundred coach services operate both domestic and international routes, mainly to Western European countries with large Serb diaspora communities.&#x20;



[Air Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Serbia) wide-body aircraft featuring [Nikola Tesla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Tesla) on its tail



[Srbijavoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srbijavoz) *Soko* high-speed train

Serbia has 3,357 km (2,086 mi) of [railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport) tracks, of which 1,313 km (816 mi) are electrified and 349 km (217 mi) are double-track.[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of-275] There are currently 183 km (114 mi) of [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) connecting three of the country's five largest cities: Belgrade, Novi Sad and Subotica.[^cite_note-276] Construction of a 212 km (132 mi) extension of the high-speed railway to the southern city of Niš, is set to commence in 2027.[^cite_note-277] Passenger rail services are operated by [Srbijavoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srbijavoz), which carried around 7.7 million passengers on intercity routes in 2025.[^cite_note-prevoz-278]&#x20;

A total of 9.3 million passengers passed through the country's [three international airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Serbia) in 2025, with [Belgrade Nikola Tesla Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_Nikola_Tesla_Airport) serving the vast majority of them.[^cite_note-279] The airport offers connections to 118 destinations across more than 40 countries, eight of which are intercontinental routes to North America, the Far East and the Middle East.[^cite_note-280] It is the hub of the national flag carrier [Air Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Serbia), which operates flights to 90 destinations in 34 countries and carried 4.5 million passengers in 2025.[^cite_note-281]&#x20;

Serbia has a developed inland water transport since there are 1,716 km (1,066 mi) of navigable [inland waterways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inland_navigation), of which 963 km (598 mi) are rivers and 673 km (418 mi) canals, all located in northern third of the country. The most important inland waterway is the [Danube](21209639.md) which connects country with the North Sea and Atlantic Ocean through the [Rhine–Main–Danube Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine%E2%80%93Main%E2%80%93Danube_Canal) as well as to the Black Sea through the [Danube–Black Sea Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danube%E2%80%93Black_Sea_Canal). Other navigable rivers include [Sava](587845.md) and [Tisza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisza). Serbian river ports handled 15 million tonnes of cargo in 2024, the largest of which are: [Novi Sad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Novi_Sad), [Pančevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan%C4%8Devo), [Smederevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smederevo), and [Prahovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prahovo).&#x20;

A total of 37.3 million tonnes of freight was transported in 2025, of which 22.7 million tonnes by road, 9.3 million tonnes by rail, 5.3 million tonnes by inland waterways, and 6,233 tonnes by air.[^cite_note-RZS2025-282]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Serbia) and [Serbs](59512.md)

According to the 2022 census, Serbia (excluding Kosovo) had a total population of 6,647,003, ranking [107th in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population).[^cite_note-Census_2022-10] It has a moderate [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_by_population_density) of 85.8 inhabitants per square kilometre.&#x20;

Serbia completed its [demographic transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_transition) by the 1970s and since the 1990s has experienced a demographic crisis characterized by below-replacement fertility, negative natural increase, population ageing, and net emigration.[^cite_note-283] In 2025, the crude [birth rate](515898.md) was 9.1 per 1,000 inhabitants, while the crude [death rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate) was 14.9 per 1,000, among [the highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_mortality_rate).[^cite_note-statrelease-284] Although the [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) of 1.6 children per woman is one of the highest in Europe, it remains well below the replacement level of 2.1.[^cite_note-statrelease-284] Serbia has a comparatively old population, with a median age of 44.0 years and 22.6% of inhabitants aged 65 or older.[^cite_note-statrelease-284] About 30% of all households consist of a single person, while only 20% consist of four or more people.[^cite_note-285] Over the past decade, Serbia has seen an average annual [net migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_migration_rate) loss of approximately 12,000 people, underscoring a persistent demographic outflow.&#x20;



Ethnic map of Serbia

[^cite_note-286]

Country is home to many different ethnic groups. The [Serbs](59512.md) are the largest ethnic group, numbering 5,360,239 people and accounting for 80.6% of the population.[^cite_note-ethnicity-287] They are the titular ethnic group of Serbia, which is constitutionally recognized as their [nation-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state). Despite a decline in recent decades, [Hungarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarians_in_Serbia) remain the largest ethnic minority, with a population of 184,442, accounting for 2.8% of the country's population and concentrated predominantly in northern [Bačka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%8Dka). [Bosniaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosniaks), numbering 153,801 and representing 2.3% of the population, are the second-largest ethnic minority, mainly inhabiting the [Sandžak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand%C5%BEak) region in the southwest of the country. The [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people_in_Serbia) population stood at 131,936, constituting 2% of the total population. All other ethnic groups individually account for less than 1% of the population, including [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians_in_Serbia) (0.9%); [Slovaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovaks_in_Serbia) and [Croats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croats_of_Serbia) (0.6% each); [Yugoslavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslavs_in_Serbia) (0.4%); [Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanians_in_Serbia), [Vlachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlachs_of_Serbia), and [Montenegrins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montenegrins) (0.3% each); [Macedonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonians_\(ethnic_group\)) and [Bulgarians](151876.md) (0.2% each); and [Rusyns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Rusyns) and [Bunjevci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunjevci) (0.1% each).&#x20;

Compared to other Eastern European countries, Serbia saw relatively low levels of [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) until the latter half of the 20th century.[^cite_note-288] Country witnessed mass labour emigration in the late 1960s and throughout the 1970s and 1980s, during which hundreds of thousands emigrated as "guest workers" to [Western Europe](33800.md), driven by unemployment and bilateral labour agreements between Yugoslavia and the respective destination countries.[^cite_note-289] This was followed in the 1990s by another wave of mass emigration, caused by political instability resulting from the [breakup of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakup_of_Yugoslavia) as well as by the economic collapse caused by [international sanctions against Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_against_Serbia_and_Montenegro).[^cite_note-290] According to recent estimates, around 800,000 Serbian citizens and Serbia-born persons live abroad, predominantly in [Europe](9239.md) and, to a much lesser extent, overseas, primarily in [North America](21139.md) and [Oceania](22621.md).[^cite_note-291]&#x20;

Parallel to the process of emigration, the country experienced [immigration](4599312.md), primarily involving ethnic Serbs from neighbouring regions and countries. Throughout the 19th century, Serbia experienced substantial immigration of ethnic Serbs from areas under Ottoman and Habsburg rule, contributing significantly to population growth. During and after World War II, tens of thousands of Serb refugees from the [Independent State of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia) arrived in Serbia, while more than 200,000 predominantly Serb settlers from Croatia and Bosnia-Herzegovina were resettled in Vojvodina under a postwar colonization programme; migration of ethnic Serbs from other Yugoslav republics to Serbia continued throughout the era of socialist Yugoslavia.[^cite_note-292] In the 1990s, the dissolution of Yugoslavia and ensuing wars led to a massive influx of ethnic Serb refugees from Croatia, Bosnia-Herzegovina, and Kosovo, with around half a million people arriving in Serbia and making it host to Europe's largest refugee population at the time.[^cite_note-293] In recent years, the country has seen a significant wave of immigration from [Russia](25391.md) following the outbreak of the war in Ukraine. Some 53,000 Russian nationals have settled in the country after being issued residence permits.[^cite_note-foreign_nationals-294] Additionally, 12,000 [Chinese](372983.md) people live in the country, mainly linked to economic activities and investments by Chinese companies in mining and manufacturing. There has been also a sizeable influx of South Asian migrants, primarily [Indians](7564733.md), who have arrived as labour migrants working on large-scale infrastructure projects and in transportation and courier services.[^cite_note-foreign_nationals-294]&#x20;

The share of the population living in areas classified as urban stood at 62% in 2024.[^cite_note-295] [Belgrade](55904.md) is the only city with more than one million inhabitants and is disproportionately larger than any other city in the country, serving as an example of a [primate city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primate_city); it is four times as populous as the second-largest city, [Novi Sad](113288.md). Consequently, Serbia has a comparatively high level of metropolisation, with almost one-fifth of the country's population living in the Belgrade urban area alone.&#x20;
