### Language

Main articles: [Languages of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Serbia) and [Serbian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_language)



Serbian is a rare example of active [digraphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digraphia), with both [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic_script) (bottom) and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaj%27s_Latin_alphabet) (top) alphabets in regular use

The official language is [Serbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_language), spoken as a mother tongue by 5,607,558 people, or 84.4% of the population, and predominant throughout most of the country.[^cite_note-299] It is the most widely spoken [South Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavic_languages) language and is mutually intelligible with other standard varieties of [Serbo-Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbo-Croatian).[^cite_note-300] Serbian is unique among European languages in maintaining active [digraphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digraphia), with both [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic_script) and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaj%27s_Latin_alphabet) alphabets in regular use. [Serbian Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Cyrillic_alphabet) was standardized in the early 19th century according to strict phonemic principles, with each sound represented by a single consistent letter. The Latin alphabet used for Serbian is based on the Czech system and maintains a one-to-one correspondence with Serbian Cyrillic, creating a parallel orthographic system. The Constitution designates Serbian Cyrillic as the official script and mandates its use in legal and administrative domains.[^cite_note-Alexander2006-301] However, legislation does not regulate script usage in standard language, leaving the choice of script in everyday life to personal preference, including in publishing, media, trade, and commerce. According to a 2014 poll, 47% of the population uses the Latin alphabet more frequently, 36% primarily uses Cyrillic, while 16% uses both alphabets equally.[^cite_note-302]&#x20;

There are 14 recognized minority languages in Serbia, mainly spoken by the country's ethnic minorities: [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language) (native to 2.5% of the population), [Bosnian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_language) (2.2%), [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_language) (1.2%), [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language) (1%), [Slovak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_language) (0.6%), [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) (0.3%), Vlach (0.3%), [Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language) (0.2%), [Rusyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusyn_language) (0.1%), [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_language) (0.1%), [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language) (0.1%), [Montenegrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montenegrin_language), [Bunjevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunjevac_dialect), and [Czech](5321.md). Bosnian, Croatian, Montenegrin, and Bunjevac are mutually intelligible with Serbian and share the same linguistic base. Recognized minority languages are granted official use in municipalities and cities where the share of the respective ethnic minority population exceeds 15%.[^cite_note-303] As of 2025, around 6% of students attend classes in recognized minority languages at all levels of education, from preschool to university.[^cite_note-304]&#x20;

Other languages have no official status and are largely spoken by immigrant communities. [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) is the most widely spoken among these, while there is also a certain presence of [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic).&#x20;

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Serbia) and [Serbian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church)



[Church of Saint Sava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Sava) in Belgrade, one of the [largest Orthodox churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_Eastern_Orthodox_church_buildings) in the world

Serbia is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) with no [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion), and [freedom of religion](46006.md) is guaranteed as a constitutional right.&#x20;

[Christianity](5211.md) is the most widely professed religion in Serbia which has been a predominantly Christian country since the [christianization of Serbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church#Christianization_of_Serbs) in the 9th century.&#x20;

The [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) is by far the largest Christian denomination and is prevalent throughout most of the country. According to the 2022 census, there were 5,387,426 adherents of Eastern Orthodoxy, accounting for 81.1% of the total population.[^cite_note-\:0-305] They are overwhelmingly ethnic Serbs, who constitute the world's westernmost predominantly Orthodox ethnic group. The [Serbian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church) is the traditional church of the country and is the sole jurisdiction for Orthodox Christians not only in the territory of Serbia but also in Bosnia-Herzegovina, Montenegro, Croatia, and Slovenia, which together constitute its canonical territory.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

[Catholicism](606848.md) is a minority Christian denomination in Serbia, with 257,269 adherents, representing 3.9% of the population.[^cite_note-\:0-305] It is primarily present among ethnic Hungarians and Croats, but also among Rusyns, who are traditionally [Greek Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Catholic_Church).[^cite_note-307]&#x20;

[Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) is another minority Christian denomination in Serbia, with 54,678 adherents accounting for 0.8% of the country's population.[^cite_note-\:0-305] Historically, Protestants in Serbia have consisted primarily of [Magisterial Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magisterial_Reformation) traditions, particularly [Lutheranism](23371382.md) and [Calvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_Christianity).[^cite_note-308] Protestantism is prevalent among ethnic Slovaks, who are predominantly Lutheran and constitute the majority of the country's Protestant population. It is also present among ethnic Hungarians, a minority of whom are Protestant, predominantly belonging to the Reformed (Calvinist) tradition.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]&#x20;

[Islam](6037917.md) is a minority religion and the second largest religion in Serbia, with nearly all country's Muslims belonging to [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam). There were 278,212 Muslims in the country, accounting for 4.2% of the total population. Islam is particularly prevalent among ethnic Bosniaks and Albanians; in addition, approximately one-quarter of the country's Roma population is Muslim.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;

A comparatively small portion of the population is [non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) or skeptical, with 1.1% identifying as [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and an additional 0.1% as [agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism).[^cite_note-\:0-305]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Serbia)



Rectorate building of the [University of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Belgrade), country's oldest university, founded in 1808

According to the 2022 census, the [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy) rate in Serbia was 99.3% while [computer literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_literacy) stood at 75.3% (complete computer literacy was 45.7% while 29.6% of persons had partial computer skills).[^cite_note-312] Same census showed the following levels of educational attainment: 22.4% of inhabitants have higher education (16.4% have [bachelor's degree](188874.md) or higher, 6% have an [associate degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_degree)), 53% have a secondary education, 17.8% have an elementary education, 5.2% have not completed elementary education and 1% were without educational attainment.[^cite_note-313]&#x20;

[Primary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_education) is compulsory and consists of preschool and eight grades of primary school. [Preschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschool) is provided for all children aged six years, after which children enroll in [primary schools](25058.md) and attend it until the age of fourteen. [Secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) includes two types of schools, gymnasiums and vocational schools. [Gymnasiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)) provide a general education, while [vocational schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_school) offer either four-year programs preparing students for a particular profession or three-year programs focused on crafts. In the 2022 [PISA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) international study evaluating educational systems, Serbia ranked 40th, 41st, and 42nd out of 81 countries in the mathematics, science, and reading performance of 15-year-old students, respectively, with scores below the OECD average.[^cite_note-314] Reflecting the performance of its high-achieving students, Serbia ranks 29th in the all-time medal table of the [International Mathematical Olympiad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Mathematical_Olympiad) with 96 medals, despite participating only since 1995.&#x20;

Following the completion of gymnasiums or four-year vocational schools, students may pursue [tertiary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education), admission to which generally requires passing the [Matura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matura), the secondary school leaving examination.[^cite_note-315] There are 17 [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University) in Serbia, eight public and nine private. The [University of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Belgrade) is the country's largest and generally regarded as its leading institution of higher education, ranking in the 401–500 band of the *[2024 Academic Ranking of World Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities)*.[^cite_note-316] In addition, there are 71 "higher schools" (colleges) offering undergraduate education leading to an associate degree.&#x20;

### Health

Main articles: [Health in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Serbia) and [Healthcare in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Serbia)



[Military Medical Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Medical_Academy_\(Serbia\)), one of major medical centers in the country

Serbia has a [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system largely financed by [public health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care). In 2023, Serbia spent 8% of its GDP on healthcare, amounting to \$6.5 billion, or \$983 per capita.[^cite_note-317] As of 2024, the country had 21,248 medical doctors (310 per 100,000 inhabitants) and 41,137 hospital beds (600 per 100,000 inhabitants).[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of-275] The public healthcare network is fairly robust and comprises 306 institutions, including five major [medical centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_medical_centre), 47 [general hospitals](18994221.md), 54 specialized hospitals as well as 151 [community health centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_health_center) providing primary healthcare.[^cite_note-hospitals-318] Alongside the public system, Serbia has a growing private healthcare sector comprising clinics and community healthcare centers, mostly operated by healthcare providers, as well as small private practices, primarily in dentistry and ophthalmology. Having emerged in the 1990s through the establishment of small private practices, the sector expanded rapidly over the past two decades with the development of clinics and community healthcare centers run by healthcare providers such as [Acibadem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ac%C4%B1badem_Healthcare_Group), MediGroup, and Euromedik.[^cite_note-319]&#x20;

In 2023, Serbia had an average [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) of 76.7 years, ranking 68th worldwide, and a low [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) rate of 4.6 deaths per 1,000 live births.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] The principal cause of death in 2024 was [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease), accounting for 47.7% of all deaths, followed by [cancer](105219.md) at 21.7%.[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of-275] According to 2024 data, 33% of the adult population was [overweight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overweight), while 24% was [obese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity).[^cite_note-322] In 2023, an estimated 39% of people aged 15 and over were smokers, the highest proportion of any European country.[^cite_note-323] Although [smoking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoking) is prohibited in most enclosed public spaces, cafés and restaurants remain exempt from the ban, making Serbia one of the few European countries where indoor smoking is still permitted. Serbia has a long-standing tradition of spa-based [rehabilitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_medicine_and_rehabilitation), with medical spas playing an important role in the treatment of chronic conditions and post-operative recovery. Rehabilitation in many medical [spas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spa) is covered by the public health insurance system, contributing to their widespread use, particularly among older population.[^cite_note-324]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Serbia)



The *[Miroslav Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miroslav_Gospel)* (1180), a 362-page illuminated manuscript

The cultural identity of Serbia was shaped by centuries of historical divisions and overlapping influences as its territory successively formed the frontier between the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and the [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity), and later the [Ottoman](22278.md) and [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy) empires.[^cite_note-325] These influences have produced a diverse cultural heritage, combining Byzantine, Central European and Balkan traditions in art, architecture, literature, music, with regional variations particularly evident between the northern Serbia (Vojvodina) which leans to the profile of Central Europe, and the central and southern Serbia with predominantly Balkan character.[^cite_note-326]&#x20;

Serbia has five [World Heritage](44940.md) sites: the early medieval capital of [Stari Ras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stari_Ras) (including the nearby 9th-century [Church of Saints Peter and Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Apostles_Peter_and_Paul,_Ras) and monasteries of [Sopoćani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sopo%C4%87ani) and [Đurđevi Stupovi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90ur%C4%91evi_stupovi)), the 12th-century [Studenica Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studenica_Monastery), the 3rd-century Roman palace complex of [Felix Romuliana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamzigrad), late medieval [stećak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ste%C4%87ak) necropolises at three sites in southwestern Serbia, and the [Medieval Monuments in Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Monuments_in_Kosovo) (comprising the Serbian Orthodox monasteries of [Visoki Dečani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visoki_De%C4%8Dani), [Gračanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gra%C4%8Danica_Monastery) and the [Patriarchate of Peć](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_\(monastery\)) as well as Serbian Orthodox church of [Our Lady of Ljeviš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Ljevi%C5%A1)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEČanak-MedićTodić2017-327] The six Serbian cultural traditions are inscribed on [World Intangible Cultural Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_elements) list: *[slava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slava_\(tradition\))* (family patron saint veneration), *[kolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolo_\(dance\))* folk dance, traditional singing accompanied by the [gusle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gusle), [Zlakusa pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlakusa_pottery), [slivovitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slivovitz)-making and associated social practices, and the [naïve painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na%C3%AFve_art) practices of [Kovačica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kova%C4%8Dica).[^cite_note-328] Four items from Serbia are inscribed on [Memory of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memory_of_the_World_Programme) programme: the 12th-century *[Miroslav Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miroslav_Gospel)* manuscript, the 1914 telegram of Austria-Hungary's declaration of war on Serbia, the archives of [Nikola Tesla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Tesla), and the archives of the First Summit of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) held in Belgrade in 1961.[^cite_note-329]&#x20;

### Art and architecture

Main articles: [Serbian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_art) and [Serbian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_architecture)



The *[White Angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Angel)* (1235), [fresco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco) at [Mileševa Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_Monastery)

The history of Serbian [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting) originates with medieval [fresco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco) painting of the Orthodox monasteries and churches, with the most notable frescoes such as the *[White Angel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Angel)* at [Mileševa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_Monastery), the *Crucifixion* at [Studenica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studenica_Monastery), and the *Dormition of the Virgin* at [Sopoćani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sopo%C4%87ani), regarded among the finest achievements of [Byzantine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art).[^cite_note-330] During [Ottoman rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Serbia), artistic production was virtually non-existent and largely continued in the [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy)-ruled regions inhabited by Serbs (present-day Vojvodina province), where [baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_painting) painters such as [Nikola Nešković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Ne%C5%A1kovi%C4%87), [Teodor Kračun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teodor_Kra%C4%8Dun) and [Jakov Orfelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakov_Orfelin) emerged in the 18th century.[^cite_note-331] The 19th century saw the development of Serbian academic painting. [Neoclassicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassicism) and [biedermeier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biedermeier) gained prominence through painters such as [Konstantin Danil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Danil), followed by [romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) of [Đura Jakšić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90ura_Jak%C5%A1i%C4%87) and [Katarina Ivanović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katarina_Ivanovi%C4%87).[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] By the late 19th and early 20th centuries, [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) was dominated by [Paja Jovanović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paja_Jovanovi%C4%87) and [Uroš Predić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87). Modern art in the 20th century saw Serbian artists actively participating in major European modern movements. [Impressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism) and [fauvism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauvism) were introduced by [Nadežda Petrović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nade%C5%BEda_Petrovi%C4%87), while [cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) and [expressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionism) were embraced by artists such as [Sava Šumanović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sava_%C5%A0umanovi%C4%87), [Milan Konjović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan_Konjovi%C4%87), and [Milena Pavlović-Barili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milena_Pavlovi%C4%87-Barili).[^cite_note-334] In the contemporary period, the art scene expanded into modern [abstraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art), [surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism), and [figurative art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Figurative_art), led by [Petar Lubarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petar_Lubarda), [Marko Čelebonović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marko_%C4%8Celebonovi%C4%87), and [Vladimir Veličković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Veli%C4%8Dkovi%C4%87). Serbia is also known for the tradition of [naïve art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na%C3%AFve_art) centered in the small town of [Kovačica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kova%C4%8Dica), whose distinctive painting school has gained international recognition.[^cite_note-335]&#x20;

The [applied arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Applied_arts) are deeply tied to Serbian traditional craftsmanship. The best-known examples include the [Pirot carpet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirot_carpet) (*Pirotski ćilim*), a flat-woven tapestry renowned for its geometric patterns, and the distinctive [Zlakusa pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlakusa_pottery), a specialized style of unglazed hand-wheel ceramics mixed with ground calcite.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] Other traditional crafts such as [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery) and [woodworking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodworking) are also integral part of country's decorative arts tradition.&#x20;

In the field of contemporary [performance art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performance_art), Serbia is globally recognized as the origin of groundbreaking experimental art, most notably through [Marina Abramović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Abramovi%C4%87), one of the world's most influential performance artists known for her pioneering body art and endurance pieces.[^cite_note-338]&#x20;

Serbia's [architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture) heritage spans from the Roman period, represented by sites such as [Felix Romuliana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamzigrad), to the medieval monasteries and fortifications that rank among the country's most important cultural monuments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETurlej2016193-339][^cite_note-340] Medieval Serbian architecture developed under strong Byzantine influence, exemplified by the monasteries of [Visoki Dečani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visoki_De%C4%8Dani), [Gračanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gra%C4%8Danica_Monastery), [Studenica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studenica_Monastery), and [Mileševa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_Monastery).[^cite_note-The_Royal_Family_of_Serbia-341] It evolved over times into the distinct, decorative [Morava style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morava_architectural_school) of the late 14th and 15th centuries, characterized by richly ornamented church façades, examples of this include [Kalenić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaleni%C4%87_Monastery), [Ravanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravanica), and [Ljubostinja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ljubostinja) monasteries. The country is dotted with many well-preserved medieval fortifications and castles such as [Smederevo Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smederevo_Fortress) (largest lowland fortress in Europe), [Manasija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasija), and [Golubac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golubac_Fortress) as well as early modern sprawling fortifications of [Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_fortress) and [Petrovaradin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrovaradin_Fortress) fortresses.[^cite_note-342] The rapid urban development of the late 19th and early 20th centuries left a rich architectural legacy, particularly in the towns of Vojvodina, where cities such as [Novi Sad](113288.md), [Subotica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subotica), [Sombor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sombor) and [Zrenjanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zrenjanin) are noted for their well-preserved historic centers featuring prominent examples of [art noveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau), [renaissance revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Revival_architecture), and [baroque revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_Revival_architecture) architecture. During the same period, Belgrade acquired many of its landmark public buildings in [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) style, while the 20th century saw the emergence of notable [modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_architecture) and [brutalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brutalist_architecture) architecture, latter most notably displayed in the planned socialist-era urbanism of [New Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Belgrade).[^cite_note-343]&#x20;

There are 31 [art museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_museum) in Serbia,[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of_2-344] including the [National Museum of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Serbia), founded in 1844, housing one of the largest art collections in the Southeast Europe.[^cite_note-345] Other art museums with internationally significant collections include the [Museum of Contemporary Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Contemporary_Art,_Belgrade) in Belgrade and the [Gallery of Matica srpska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallery_of_Matica_srpska) in Novi Sad.&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Serbian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_literature)

Serbian literature originated in the [medieval period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and was predominantly religious in character, consisting of gospels, hagiographies, psalters, sermons, and theological writings. [Saint Sava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Sava) and [Jefimija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefimija) laid the foundations of Serbian literary tradition.[^cite_note-346] During the centuries of [Ottoman rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Serbia), oral literature and [epic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_epic_poetry) became central to preserving historical memory and cultural identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDragnich199429–30-347] The oldest known, entirely fictional poems make up the "Non-historic cycle", which is followed by the "Kosovo cycles" (poems inspired by events before, during, and after the [Battle of Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kosovo)) forming the most significant body of South Slavic [oral literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_literature).[^cite_note-CaseNaimark2003-348]&#x20;



[Ivo Andrić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Andri%C4%87), winner of the [1961 Nobel Prize in Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Nobel_Prize_in_Literature)

Modern Serbian literature emerged during the 19th-century [national revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Revival) through the language reforms of [Vuk Karadžić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuk_Karad%C5%BEi%C4%87), whose standardization of the Serbian literary language and collection of folk poetry profoundly shaped national culture. [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) flourished through poets such as [Branko Radičević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branko_Radi%C4%8Devi%C4%87) and [Laza Kostić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laza_Kosti%C4%87), giving way in the later 19th century to a strong [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(art_movement\)) movement featuring prose writers like [Borisav Stanković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borisav_Stankovi%C4%87) and [Stevan Sremac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevan_Sremac).[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]&#x20;

In the early 20th century, [modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) and [symbolist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(movement\)) poetry was represented by [Jovan Dučić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jovan_Du%C4%8Di%C4%87), [Milan Rakić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan_Raki%C4%87), and [Vladislav Petković Dis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Petkovi%C4%87_Dis). During the [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period), Serbian literature became increasingly experimental, with the [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) movement producing influential writers such as [Miloš Crnjanski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milo%C5%A1_Crnjanski) and [Rastko Petrović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastko_Petrovi%C4%87). In the second half of the 20th century, Serbian literature gained global recognition, marked by [Ivo Andrić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Andri%C4%87) winning the [Nobel Prize](23385442.md) in 1961 for works such as *[The Bridge on the Drina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bridge_on_the_Drina)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnel2004209-351] Other major postwar figures include [Borislav Pekić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borislav_Peki%C4%87), [Meša Selimović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Me%C5%A1a_Selimovi%C4%87), [Danilo Kiš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danilo_Ki%C5%A1), and [Dobrica Ćosić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobrica_%C4%86osi%C4%87).[^cite_note-352]&#x20;

Among contemporary writers, [Milorad Pavić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milorad_Pavi%C4%87) is the most critically acclaimed and internationally recognized. His *[Dictionary of the Khazars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_the_Khazars)* is widely regarded as one of the defining works of [postmodern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodern_literature) literature, became an international bestseller during the late 1980s and 1990s, and has been translated into 40 languages.[^cite_note-353] Today, Serbian literature remains internationally visible through novelists [Svetislav Basara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svetislav_Basara), [David Albahari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Albahari), and [Zoran Živković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoran_%C5%BDivkovi%C4%87_\(writer\)), whose science fiction works have been published in 20 languages.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]&#x20;

Serbia has a dense network of public libraries, with more than 535 institutions nationwide.[^cite_note-statistical_yearbook_of_2-344] The largest are the [National Library of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Serbia) in Belgrade, with 6.5 million items, and [Library of Matica srpska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matica_srpska) in Novi Sad, founded in 1826 and the oldest Serbian cultural institution, with around 4 million items.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] Book publishing remains active and the annual [Belgrade Book Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_Book_Fair), attracting more than 200,000 visitors, is the country's largest cultural event.[^cite_note-358] The highlight of the literary scene is awarding of [NIN Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIN_Award), given annually since 1954 for the best newly published novel in Serbian.[^cite_note-359]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Serbia)

Music in Serbia encompasses medieval traditions, classical composition, folk music and a diverse popular music scene.&#x20;

Traditional Serbian music includes a variety of vocal and instrumental forms. The [gusle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gusle), a one-string bowed instrument, has accompanied the singing of epic poetry since the Middle Ages, with records of its use at the Serbian royal court in the early 13th century. Performed by *guslari*, these poems preserve historical events and mythology and remain among the best-known expressions of Serbian oral tradition.[^cite_note-360]&#x20;

Composer and musicologist [Stevan Mokranjac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevan_Mokranjac) is regarded as the founder of modern Serbian [art music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_music), having played a key role in the development of Serbian national musical expression in the late 19th and early 20th centuries.[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] The first generation of Serbian composers, including [Petar Konjović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petar_Konjovi%C4%87), [Stevan Hristić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevan_Hristi%C4%87) and [Miloje Milojević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miloje_Milojevi%C4%87), continued the national tradition while introducing modern influences, particularly from [Impressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism_in_music).[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] Among contemporary composers, [Isidora Žebeljan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isidora_%C5%BDebeljan) is the most internationally performed Serbian composer of the 21st century, acclaimed for her operas, orchestral works, and chamber music that combine Balkan folk elements with contemporary compositional techniques.[^cite_note-365] Among recognised performers are violinists [Stefan Milenković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan_Milenkovich) and [Nemanja Radulović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemanja_Radulovi%C4%87), both known for their successful international careers.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] Serbia's classical music institutions include three opera houses and four symphony orchestras, while the [Belgrade Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_Music_Festival) is among the leading classical music festivals in Southeastern Europe.&#x20;

[Balkan brass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_brass), or *truba* ("trumpet"), emerged in central Serbia during the late nineteenth century following the introduction of military brass orchestras, eventually developing into a distinctive musical tradition associated with celebrations. The genre gained international recognition through performers such as [Boban Marković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boban_Markovi%C4%87), while the work of Belgrade-based composer and musician [Goran Bregović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goran_Bregovi%C4%87), whose compositions frequently incorporate Balkan brass traditions, has further popularized the genre among international audiences.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;



Main stage at [Exit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exit_\(festival\)) music festival in Novi Sad

[Serbian folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_folk_music) has remained the country's most popular musical genre, evolving from traditional rural music into commercially successful popular folk. The genre gained wider popularity during the [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period) through performers such as [Sofka Nikolić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofka_Nikoli%C4%87), the earliest major star of popular music in Serbia, and was later continued by prominent singers including [Predrag Živković Tozovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predrag_%C5%BDivkovi%C4%87_Tozovac), [Toma Zdravković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toma_Zdravkovi%C4%87), [Lepa Lukić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepa_Luki%C4%87), and [Miroslav Ilić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miroslav_Ili%C4%87).[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370]&#x20;

[Turbo-folk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbo-folk), a genre combining traditional folk music with pop, dance and electronic influences, emerged in Serbia during the late 1980s and early 1990s.[^cite_note-371] Despite frequent cultural and political controversy, it became the most commercially successful genre of Serbian popular music, enjoying an immense popularity throughout the former Yugoslavia as well as in Bulgaria.[^cite_note-372] Notable performers include [Ceca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceca_\(singer\)), [Lepa Brena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepa_Brena), [Aca Lukas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aca_Lukas), [Saša Matić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%C5%A1a_Mati%C4%87), and recent acts such as [Aleksandra Prijović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandra_Prijovi%C4%87) and [Tea Tairović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Tairovi%C4%87).[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374]&#x20;

[Serbian pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_pop) has achieved certain international recognition through the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md). [Željko Joksimović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDeljko_Joksimovi%C4%87) finished second at the [2004 contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2004), while [Marija Šerifović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marija_%C5%A0erifovi%C4%87) won the [2007 contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2007), securing Serbia's first Eurovision victory. The most prominent and enduring figures of Serbian pop music are singer [Zdravko Čolić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zdravko_%C4%8Coli%C4%87) and singer-songwriter [Đorđe Balašević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90or%C4%91e_Bala%C5%A1evi%C4%87), both of whom have remained popular across multiple generations.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]&#x20;

[Serbian rock music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music_in_Serbia) developed within the broader Yugoslav cultural space and formed an important part of the [Yugoslav rock scene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music_in_Yugoslavia) from the 1960s onwards, producing influential bands including [Riblja Čorba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riblja_%C4%8Corba), [Bajaga i Instruktori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajaga_i_Instruktori), [Ekatarina Velika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekatarina_Velika), [Smak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smak) and [Električni Orgazam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elektri%C4%8Dni_Orgazam).[^cite_note-377] Although several major acts managed to sustain their popularity its mainstream popularity has declined in recent decades.[^cite_note-378]&#x20;

[Serbian hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_hip-hop) replaced rock as the country's most popular [urban music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_contemporary_music) genre, with new generations of artists achieving significant popularity through digital platforms. It emerged during the late 1980s and gained wider popularity from the 1990s onwards, producing influential performers across underground and commercial styles, including [Gru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gru_\(rapper\)), [Bad Copy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Copy), and [Beogradski Sindikat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beogradski_Sindikat). In recent years, [trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trap_music) has become particularly popular among younger audiences with acts such as [Rasta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasta_\(singer\)), [Devito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devito_\(rapper\)), and [Voyage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyage_\(rapper\)).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]&#x20;

Serbia hosts numerous internationally recognized music festivals. The [Guča Trumpet Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu%C4%8Da_Trumpet_Festival) in the small town of [Guča](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu%C4%8Da), is the world's largest celebration of brass band music, with attendance figures reaching around 300,000 visitors in peak years. [Exit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exit_\(festival\)) in Novi Sad is one of Europe's best-known contemporary music festivals and has attracted over 200,000 visitors in some editions.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] Other major events include the [Nišville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1ville) jazz festival in Niš and [Gitarijada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gitarijada) in [Zaječar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaje%C4%8Dar), one of Europe's longest-running rock festivals. Following Serbia's victory at the 2007 contest, Belgrade hosted the [2008 Eurovision Song Contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2008).&#x20;

### Cinema and theatre

Main articles: [Cinema of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Serbia) and [Theatre of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Serbia)

Serbia has a rich cinematic tradition dating back to 1911, when the first Serbian feature film, *[The Life and Deeds of Karađorđe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Life_and_Deeds_of_the_Immortal_Leader_Kara%C4%91or%C4%91e)*, was released, making it one of the earliest feature films produced in Central and Eastern Europe.[^cite_note-383] The [Yugoslav Film Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Film_Archive), with more than 100,000 film prints, is among the five largest film archives in the world.[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385]&#x20;

Country has one of Europe's most dynamic smaller film industries.[^cite_note-386] In 2025, 36 feature films were produced, with domestic productions attracting a comparatively large audience and accounting for around one-fifth of the more than 4 million cinema admissions recorded.[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] The film industry is heavily subsidised by the state, mainly through the Film Centre of Serbia, which provides grants for film production.[^cite_note-390]&#x20;



Film director [Emir Kusturica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir_Kusturica), two-time winner of the [Palme d'Or](612946.md) at the [Cannes Film Festival](101460.md)

Serbian filmmakers have received widespread international recognition across feature, short, and documentary filmmaking. In feature films, [Emir Kusturica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir_Kusturica) is one of the few directors to have won two [Palmes d'Or](612946.md) at the [Cannes Film Festival](101460.md), while [Želimir Žilnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDelimir_%C5%BDilnik) won the [Golden Bear](21547166.md) at the [Berlin International Film Festival](18948039.md).[^cite_note-391] [Stefan Arsenijević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan_Arsenijevi%C4%87) received the Golden Bear for Best Short Film, while [Mila Turajlić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mila_Turajli%C4%87) won the top award at the [International Documentary Film Festival Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Documentary_Film_Festival_Amsterdam), demonstrating Serbia's international recognition across different cinematic formats.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] Serbian-American screenwriter [Steve Tesich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Tesich) won the [Academy Award for Best Original Screenplay](47125790.md) in 1979 for *[Breaking Away](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breaking_Away)*.[^cite_note-people-394] Among Serbia's most acclaimed screen actors are [Miki Manojlović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miki_Manojlovi%C4%87), whose career has included numerous internationally recognized productions, and [Milena Dravić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milena_Dravi%C4%87), one of the country's most celebrated actresses, who received the [Cannes Film Festival Award for Best Actress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes_Film_Festival_Award_for_Best_Actress) in 1980.&#x20;

The [FEST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FEST_\(film_festival\)) was a prominent film festival during the 1970s and 1980s, presenting major international films and filmmakers. Although its international profile has largely declined since the 1990s, it remains an important cultural event in Serbia.[^cite_note-395]&#x20;

Country has a significant theatrical tradition dating to the early 19th century. [Joakim Vujić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joakim_Vuji%C4%87) is regarded as the founder of modern Serbian theatre, while [Princely Serbian Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_Serbian_Theatre) in [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac), established in 1835, is the country's oldest continuously operating theatre.[^cite_note-Joakim-396] Serbian theatre has been shaped by playwrights such as [Jovan Sterija Popović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jovan_Sterija_Popovi%C4%87) and [Branislav Nušić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branislav_Nu%C5%A1i%C4%87), while contemporary theatre is represented by acclaimed writers such as [Dušan Kovačević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du%C5%A1an_Kova%C4%8Devi%C4%87) and [Biljana Srbljanović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biljana_Srbljanovi%C4%87).[^cite_note-397] The [Belgrade International Theatre Festival – BITEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_International_Theatre_Festival), founded in 1967, is prominent for presenting contemporary and experimental international theatre.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] The [Sterijino pozorje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterijino_pozorje) festival, established in 1956, is dedicated to Serbian drama and the promotion of domestic theatrical production.&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Serbia)

Freedom of the media is guaranteed by the Constitution of Serbia.[^cite_note-400] However, country is ranked 96th out of 180 countries in the 2025 [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) report.[^cite_note-401] The report noted that media outlets and journalists continue to face partisan and government pressure over editorial policies.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403]&#x20;



[Avala Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avala_Tower), a 204-meter-high television and radio transmission tower

[Television](29831.md) remains a traditional source of news and entertainment. Although Serbia completed the transition to [digital terrestrial television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_terrestrial_television) in 2015, terrestrial broadcasting accounts for only a marginal share of television reception, as approximately 94% of households receive television through subscription-based platforms ([cable television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television), [IPTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPTV), and [direct-to-home satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_television) services), making Serbia one of the world's most highly penetrated [pay television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pay_television) markets.[^cite_note-404] In addition to carrying three channels of the public broadcaster, [Radio Television of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_of_Serbia) (RTS), as well as free-to-air nationwide commercial, regional, and local channels, these platforms distribute dozens of additional domestic commercial channels, ranging from general-interest networks to specialized services devoted to sports, news, music, and documentaries. They also provide access to numerous international channels, some of which are available with Serbian subtitles.&#x20;

[Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio) occupies a more limited role in Serbia's media landscape. The public broadcaster, RTS, operates the nationwide [Radio Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Belgrade) network of three stations, alongside numerous commercial national, regional, and local radio stations. In addition to [FM broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FM_broadcasting), the country's [digital audio broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_Audio_Broadcasting) network provides coverage to more than 90% of the population.[^cite_note-405]&#x20;

Print media in Serbia has undergone a significant decline in last two decades, with newspaper and magazine circulation falling sharply as audiences have increasingly shifted to digital platforms. *[Politika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politika)*, founded in 1904, is the oldest continuously published daily newspaper in the Balkans and is widely regarded as Serbia's [newspaper of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_of_record).[^cite_note-406] Serbian [newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper) today operate primarily through their online editions, which reach substantially larger audiences than their printed versions. Leading tabloid newspapers such as *[Blic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blic)* and *[Kurir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurir)* are among Serbia's most visited general news sites.[^cite_note-407] In contrast to the global trend among newspapers toward adopting digital subscription models, Serbian newspapers generally rely on advertising and free access rather than paid subscriptions. The [magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magazine) market has been even more affected, with numerous titles no longer being published, while those that remain have generally experienced rapidly declining circulation. Among the magazines that have retained a relatively strong readership is the popular science magazine *[Politikin Zabavnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politikin_Zabavnik)*, founded in 1939. Only a limited number of magazines, such as the news weeklies *[Nedeljnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nedeljnik)* and *[Vreme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vreme)*, have developed significant digital subscriber bases.[^cite_note-408]&#x20;

[Social media](5897742.md) play an increasingly significant role in Serbia's media environment, with global platforms such as [X](9988187.md) widely used for accessing news, sharing information, and engaging in public discourse.[^cite_note-409]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Serbian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_cuisine)

Serbian cuisine is largely heterogeneous in a way characteristic of the Balkans, with heavy Central European, Oriental, and Mediterranean influences. It features dishes typical of [Turkish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_cuisine) as well as cuisines originating from [Central Europe](5188.md), especially [Hungarian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_cuisine). Food is very important in Serbian social life, particularly during religious holidays such as [Christmas](6237.md), [Easter](9325.md), and feast days i.e. *[slava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slava_\(tradition\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeynink2011330-410]&#x20;

Staples of the Serbian diet include bread, meat, fruits, vegetables, and dairy products. Bread plays an important role in Serbian cuisine and features prominently in religious rituals. Meat is widely consumed, while fish is eaten to a lesser extent. The southern city of [Leskovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leskovac) is host to [Roštiljijada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ro%C5%A1tiljijada), one of the largest barbecue festivals in Europe.[^cite_note-Xinhua_2017-411]&#x20;



The traditional Serbian Christmas table features [roast pork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_pork_dishes), hearty soup, [crescent rolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croissant), and [Olivier salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_salad), accompanied by wine or [rakija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakia).

Serbian specialties include [ćevapčići](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86evapi) (grilled and seasoned caseless sausages made from minced meat), [pljeskavica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pljeskavica) (grilled spiced meat patty made from a mixture of pork, beef, and lamb), [Karađorđeva šnicla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara%C4%91or%C4%91eva_%C5%A1nicla) (veal or pork schnitzel that is stuffed with kaymak), [gibanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibanica) (cheese pie), [burek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%B6rek) (baked pastry made from a thin flaky dough that is stuffed with meat or cheese), [sarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_leaves) (stuffed leaves), [punjena paprika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_peppers) (stuffed peppers), [đuveč](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghivetch) (meat and vegetable stew), [pasulj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasulj) (bean stew), [podvarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podvarak) (roast meat with sauerkraut), [ajvar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajvar) (roasted red pepper spread), [kajmak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaymak) (dairy product similar to clotted cream), [čvarci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Cvarci) (variant of pork rinds), [proja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proja) (cornbread), and [kačamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka%C4%8Damak) (maize porridge).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeynink2011329–330-412]&#x20;

Serbians claim their country as the birthplace of [rakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rakia) (*rakija*), a highly alcoholic fruit brandy, although it is found in various forms throughout the Balkans. [Slivovitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slivovitz#Serbia,_Bosnia,_Croatia_and_Slovenia) (*šljivovica*), or plum brandy, is Serbia's national drink and its traditional rakia, distilled from [plums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum), the country's national fruit.[^cite_note-413] Serbia is the world's largest exporter of Slivovitz and the second-largest plum producer. Slivovitz holds a prominent place in Serbian culture: it is served as an aperitif, offered at the start or end of meals, and present at every major life event (birth, baptism, wedding, funeral) as well as at *slava*.[^cite_note-Mennell-383-414]&#x20;

Serbia also has a long winemaking tradition, dating back to Roman times in the 3rd century AD.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416] Serbian wines are produced in 22 viticulture regions, the most important ones being [Fruška Gora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fru%C5%A1ka_Gora), [Šumadija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0umadija), [Aleksandrovačka župa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDupa_\(disambiguation\)#Places), and [Vršac Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vr%C5%A1ac_Mountains).[^cite_note-417] Besides rakia and wine, [beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer) is a very popular alcoholic beverage in the country with [pale lagers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_lagers) being the traditional choice for Serbians.[^cite_note-The_Guardian_2006-418][^cite_note-Brewer_World-Everything_about_beer_is_here_2021-419] The most popular domestic brands of beer are [Jelen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jelen_\(beer\)) and [Zaječarsko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaje%C4%8Darsko_pivo).&#x20;

As in the rest of the Balkans, [coffee culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_culture_in_the_former_Yugoslavia) is an important cultural and social practice and Serbian coffee (a local variant of [Turkish coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_coffee)) is the most commonly consumed non-alcoholic beverage.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Serbia](10134360.md)

Despite its relatively small population, Serbia has established an international reputation for producing world-class athletes and successful national teams across a wide range of sports.&#x20;

Football, basketball, and tennis are the three most popular sports in Serbia. According to a 2025 nationwide survey, 37% of respondents named [football](10568.md) as their favourite sport, followed by [basketball](3921.md) (33%) and [tennis](29773.md) (13%).[^cite_note-422]&#x20;





Tennis player [Novak Djokovic](16100029.md) (left), the record holder with 24 [Grand Slam titles](2092499.md); basketball player [Nikola Jokić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Joki%C4%87) (right), a three-time [NBA Most Valuable Player](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_Most_Valuable_Player).

Much of Serbia's sporting culture is centered on historic multi-sport clubs, particularly [Red Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SD_Crvena_zvezda) and [Partizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSD_Partizan), whose sections compete in various sports. Their various sections have achieved sustained success across numerous sports, including Red Star's triumph in football's European Cup in 1991, the predecessor of today's [UEFA Champions League](44220.md), and Partizan's European Champions Cup title in basketball in 1992, the predecessor of the modern [EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague).&#x20;

Football attracts the largest spectator audiences and has the highest number of registered participants.[^cite_note-423] Serbia's two leading football clubs, [Red Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Star_Belgrade) and [Partizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FK_Partizan), have storied histories, with Red Star winning the European Cup in 1991, while Partizan became the first club from Eastern Europe to reach the competition's final in 1966. Their rivalry, known as the "[Eternal derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_derby_\(Serbia\))", ranks among Europe's best-known and fiercest football rivalries, and a defining feature of the country's football culture.[^cite_note-424] The [national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia_national_football_team) has qualified for three of the last four [World Cups](11370.md) but lacks significant success.&#x20;

Basketball is one of Serbia's greatest sporting strengths, with the country regarded as one of the world's leading basketball nations. The [men's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia_men%27s_national_basketball_team) has won two [World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Basketball_World_Cup), three [EuroBasket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket) titles, two Olympic silver medals, and a bronze medal in 2024. The [women's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia_women%27s_national_basketball_team) has won two [EuroBasket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_Women) titles and an Olympic bronze medal. A total of 34 Serbian players have played in the [NBA](22093.md), the fourth-highest number of any country outside the United States, including [Nikola Jokić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikola_Joki%C4%87), a three-time [NBA Most Valuable Player](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_Most_Valuable_Player_Award).[^cite_note-425]&#x20;

The recent success of Serbian tennis players has led to a surge in the sport's popularity in Serbia. [Novak Djokovic](16100029.md), widely regarded as the greatest tennis player in history,[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] has won a record 24 [Grand Slam](197638.md) titles, a record 40 [Masters](30872553.md), a record seven [year-end championships](1209231.md), and an Olympic gold medal; he has been ranked as the world No. 1 for a record 428 weeks and finished as the year-end No. 1 a record eight times.&#x20;

Serbia is also among the world's leading nations in [water polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo), with the [men's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbia_men%27s_national_water_polo_team) winning three [Olympic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo_at_the_Summer_Olympics) gold medals, three [World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_polo_at_the_World_Aquatics_Championships), and seven [European Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Water_Polo_Championship).[^cite_note-428] The country's [volleyball](32558.md) teams have likewise achieved major international success, including Olympic and World Championship medals for both men and women.[^cite_note-429]&#x20;
